=== SOURCE CODE (cpp) ===

#include <chrono>
#include <ctime>
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>

#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class Chat {
public:
    struct Message {
        std::string sender;
        std::string receiver;
        std::string message;
        std::string timestamp;
    };

    bool add_user(const std::string& username);
    bool remove_user(const std::string& username);
    bool send_message(const std::string& sender, const std::string& receiver, const std::string& message);
    std::vector<Message> get_messages(const std::string& username) const;
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Message>> get_users() const;
    std::string get_current_time() const;

private:
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Message>> users;
};

bool Chat::add_user(const std::string& username) {
    if (users.find(username) != users.end()) {
        return false;
    }
    users[username] = {};
    return true;
}

bool Chat::remove_user(const std::string& username) {
    return users.erase(username) > 0;
}

bool Chat::send_message(const std::string& sender, const std::string& receiver, const std::string& message) {
    if (users.find(sender) == users.end() || users.find(receiver) == users.end()) {
        return false;
    }

    std::string timestamp = get_current_time();
    Message message_info = { sender, receiver, message, timestamp };
    users[sender].push_back(message_info);
    users[receiver].push_back(message_info);
    return true;
}

std::vector<Chat::Message> Chat::get_messages(const std::string& username) const {
    if (users.find(username) == users.end()) {
        return {};
    }
    return users.at(username);
}

std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Chat::Message>> Chat::get_users() const {
    return users;
}

std::string Chat::get_current_time() const {
    auto now = std::chrono::system_clock::now();
    std::time_t now_c = std::chrono::system_clock::to_time_t(now);
    std::tm now_tm = *std::localtime(&now_c);

    std::ostringstream oss;
    oss << std::put_time(&now_tm, "%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S");
    return oss.str();
}

You are an expert programmer. Translate the following cpp code to py.
- Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions.

```cpp

#include <chrono>
#include <ctime>
#include <iomanip>
#include <sstream>

#include <string>
#include <unordered_map>
#include <vector>

class Chat {
public:
    struct Message {
        std::string sender;
        std::string receiver;
        std::string message;
        std::string timestamp;
    };

    bool add_user(const std::string& username);
    bool remove_user(const std::string& username);
    bool send_message(const std::string& sender, const std::string& receiver, const std::string& message);
    std::vector<Message> get_messages(const std::string& username) const;
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Message>> get_users() const;
    std::string get_current_time() const;

private:
    std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Message>> users;
};

bool Chat::add_user(const std::string& username) {
    if (users.find(username) != users.end()) {
        return false;
    }
    users[username] = {};
    return true;
}

bool Chat::remove_user(const std::string& username) {
    return users.erase(username) > 0;
}

bool Chat::send_message(const std::string& sender, const std::string& receiver, const std::string& message) {
    if (users.find(sender) == users.end() || users.find(receiver) == users.end()) {
        return false;
    }

    std::string timestamp = get_current_time();
    Message message_info = { sender, receiver, message, timestamp };
    users[sender].push_back(message_info);
    users[receiver].push_back(message_info);
    return true;
}

std::vector<Chat::Message> Chat::get_messages(const std::string& username) const {
    if (users.find(username) == users.end()) {
        return {};
    }
    return users.at(username);
}

std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Chat::Message>> Chat::get_users() const {
    return users;
}

std::string Chat::get_current_time() const {
    auto now = std::chrono::system_clock::now();
    std::time_t now_c = std::chrono::system_clock::to_time_t(now);
    std::tm now_tm = *std::localtime(&now_c);

    std::ostringstream oss;
    oss << std::put_time(&now_tm, "%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S");
    return oss.str();
}

```

```python
import datetime

class Chat:
    def __init__(self):
        self.users = {}

    def add_user(self, username):
        if username in self.users:
            return False
        self.users[username] = []
        return True

    def remove_user(self, username):
        if username in self.users:
            del self.users[username]
            return True
        return False

    def send_message(self, sender, receiver, message):
        if sender not in self.users or receiver not in self.users:
            return False
        timestamp = self.get_current_time()
        message_info = {
            "sender": sender,
            "receiver": receiver,
            "message": message,
            "timestamp": timestamp,
        }
        self.users[sender].append(message_info)
        self.users[receiver].append(message_info)
        return True

    def get_messages(self, username):
        if username not in self.users:
            return []
        return [dict(m) for m in self.users[username]]

    def get_users(self):
        return {user: [dict(m) for m in msgs] for user, msgs in self.users.items()}

    def get_current_time(self):
        return datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
```

Let's write final code:

```python
import datetime

class Chat:
    class Message:
        def __init__(self, sender, receiver, message, timestamp):
            self.sender = sender
            self.receiver = receiver
            self.message = message
            self.timestamp = timestamp

    def __init__(self):
        self._users = {}

    def add_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            return False
        self._users[username] = []
        return True

    def remove_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            del self._users[username]
            return True
        return False

    def send_message(self, sender, receiver, message):
        if sender not in self._users or receiver not in self._users:
            return False
        timestamp = self.get_current_time()
        msg = Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp)
        self._users[sender].append(msg)
        self._users[receiver].append(Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp))
        return True

    def get_messages(self, username):
        if username not in self._users:
            return []
        return [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp) for m in self._users[username]]

    def get_users(self):
        return {
            user: [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp) for m in msgs]
            for user, msgs in self._users.items()
        }

    def get_current_time(self):
        return datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
```

Need to check if `get_users` returns a dict with new lists and new Message objects. Yes.

One subtlety: In C++, `get_users` returns a copy of the map. If a user is removed after calling get_users, the returned map still contains that user. Our dict comprehension creates a new dict, so yes.

Another subtlety: In C++, `get_messages` returns a copy of vector. If a new message is sent after calling get_messages, the returned vector doesn't include it. Our list comprehension creates new list, so yes.

Potential issue: In C++, `Message` fields are public and mutable. Our Message fields are mutable. Good.

Potential issue: In C++, `Message` has no explicit constructor, so aggregate initialization. In Python, our constructor requires all four args. Good.

Potential issue: In C++, `std::unordered_map` doesn't preserve order. Python dict preserves insertion order. If code relies on iteration order of get_users, C++ order is unspecified, so any order is acceptable. Python order is deterministic but not guaranteed by C++ contract. Fine.

Potential issue: If username is not a string? C++ requires std::string. Python accepts any hashable. Not a concern.

Potential issue: If message is not a string? C++ std::string. Python accepts any. Not a concern.

Potential issue: `get_current_time` in C++ uses localtime, which is not thread-safe. Python datetime.now is thread-safe. Fine.

Potential issue: Timezone: C++ localtime uses the process's local timezone. Python datetime.now uses system local time. Same.

Potential issue: `std::put_time` with "%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S" may output year with leading zeros? Yes, %Y is 4-digit year. Python same.

Potential issue: If system clock has nanoseconds, C++ truncates to seconds. Python datetime.now has microsecond, but strftime without %f truncates? Actually strftime ignores microseconds, so seconds. Good.

Potential issue: C++ `std::chrono::system_clock::now()` returns time since epoch, then `to_time_t` may round or truncate? It typically truncates towards epoch. Python datetime.now() returns current time with microsecond; strftime without %f yields second precision. Good.

Now, consider if we should use `self._users` or `self.users`. The C++ private member is named `users`. In Python, using `_users` changes the attribute name but it's private. The public API methods remain. The prompt says "Keep behavior identical" - external code cannot access private in C++, so no external code should rely on `users`. Using `_users` is fine. But if someone does `chat._users` in Python, they can access; but that's not part of API. If we used `self.users`, it's more directly matching but also public. I think `_users` is better for encapsulation. However, the original C++ code's private member name is `users`; if the translation is expected to be a direct mapping, maybe they'd expect `self.users`. But since it's private, it doesn't matter. Let's think: In Python, there is no private, but using `_users` is idiomatic. The prompt says "Use idiomatic py only when it does not change behavior." Using `_users` doesn't change behavior of public API. So okay.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with using `Chat.Message` inside class methods before the class is fully defined. In Python, the class body is executed, and methods are defined with references to `Chat` resolved at call time, not definition time. So `Chat.Message` is fine. Alternatively, use `self.Message` but `self` is instance. `Chat.Message` is fine.

Potential issue: In Python, if the class is subclassed, `Chat.Message` always refers to base class's nested Message. In C++, `Chat::Message` is a nested type, and if you subclass Chat, the nested type is inherited? Actually in C++, nested types are members of the class, and derived classes can access them, but `Chat::Message` refers to the base's nested type. If a derived class defines its own Message, it shadows. In Python, `Chat.Message` is a class attribute, and if subclass defines its own Message, `Chat.Message` still refers to base. But methods in base use `Chat.Message`, so they always use base's Message. In C++, the methods in base use `Chat::Message` (or just `Message` inside the class), which refers to the nested type in the base class. If derived class defines a nested type with the same name, it doesn't affect base methods. So using `Chat.Message` is correct. If we used `self.Message`, it would be overridable by subclass, which would be a behavior difference. So `Chat.Message` is better.

Potential issue: In C++, `Message` is a struct nested in `Chat`. In Python, `Chat.Message` is a class attribute. Good.

Potential issue: In C++, `get_users` returns `std::unordered_map<std::string, std::vector<Message>>` by value. In Python, our `get_users` returns a dict. If a user wants to check type, it's different. But that's inherent.

Potential issue: In C++, `get_messages` returns `std::vector<Message>`. In Python, list. Good.

In Python, `copy.deepcopy(chat)` would deep copy. But we don't need to implement `__copy__` or `__deepcopy__`. The prompt doesn't mention copying. So fine.

The prompt says "Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions." If someone writes Python code that accesses `_users`, it's not equivalent to C++ code because C++ code wouldn't compile. But we can't prevent. Using `_users` is fine.

Potential issue: In C++, `std::localtime` returns a pointer to a static `std::tm` object, and we copy it. Python no.

Potential issue: In C++, `std::chrono::system_clock::now()` returns a time point with duration since epoch. `to_time_t` converts to `time_t`, which is usually an integer representing seconds. Python `datetime.now()` has microsecond precision, but `strftime` without `%f` truncates to seconds. So the returned string has second precision. Good.

Potential issue: In C++, if the system clock has a resolution finer than seconds, `to_time_t` truncates or rounds? It typically truncates. Python `datetime.now()` returns microseconds, but `strftime` ignores them. So the string is the same as the truncated second. Good.

Potential issue: In C++, if the system clock is set to a time with a leap second, `strftime` may output 60 for seconds. Python may output 60? Rare. Not relevant.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import datetime

class Chat:
    class Message:
        def __init__(self, sender, receiver, message, timestamp):
            self.sender = sender
            self.receiver = receiver
            self.message = message
            self.timestamp = timestamp

    def __init__(self):
        self._users = {}

    def add_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            return False
        self._users[username] = []
        return True

    def remove_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            del self._users[username]
            return True
        return False

    def send_message(self, sender, receiver, message):
        if sender not in self._users or receiver not in self._users:
            return False
        timestamp = self.get_current_time()
        self._users[sender].append(Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp))
        self._users[receiver].append(Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp))
        return True

    def get_messages(self, username):
        if username not in self._users:
            return []
        return [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp)
                for m in self._users[username]]

    def get_users(self):
        return {
            user: [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp)
                   for m in msgs]
            for user, msgs in self._users.items()
        }

    def get_current_time(self):
        return datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (py) ===
import datetime

class Chat:
    class Message:
        def __init__(self, sender, receiver, message, timestamp):
            self.sender = sender
            self.receiver = receiver
            self.message = message
            self.timestamp = timestamp

    def __init__(self):
        self._users = {}

    def add_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            return False
        self._users[username] = []
        return True

    def remove_user(self, username):
        if username in self._users:
            del self._users[username]
            return True
        return False

    def send_message(self, sender, receiver, message):
        if sender not in self._users or receiver not in self._users:
            return False
        timestamp = self.get_current_time()
        self._users[sender].append(Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp))
        self._users[receiver].append(Chat.Message(sender, receiver, message, timestamp))
        return True

    def get_messages(self, username):
        if username not in self._users:
            return []
        return [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp)
                for m in self._users[username]]

    def get_users(self):
        return {
            user: [Chat.Message(m.sender, m.receiver, m.message, m.timestamp)
                   for m in msgs]
            for user, msgs in self._users.items()
        }

    def get_current_time(self):
        return datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
